          (function() {
            try {
              // Capture referrer - try document.referrer first, then fall back to __previousPageUrl
              var referrerSource = document.referrer || window.__previousPageUrl || '';

                window.digitalData.page.pageInfo.referringUrl = referrerSource;

                // Parse referringPageName from referrer URL
                try {
                  const referrerUrl = new URL(referrerSource);
                  const hostname = referrerUrl.hostname.toLowerCase();
                  const isF5Domain = 
                    hostname === 'f5.com' ||
                    hostname.endsWith('.f5.com') ||
                    hostname === 'localhost';
                  
                  if (isF5Domain) {
                    // Internal F5 referrer - use F5 page name format
                    // Example: "https://www.f5.com/labs/articles/my-article" → "www:f5.labs.v2:labs:articles:my-article"
                    const subDomain = hostname.split('.')[0] || 'www';
                    const pathParts = referrerUrl.pathname.split('/').filter(Boolean);
                    
                    if (pathParts.length > 0) {
                      const primaryCategory = pathParts[0];
                      let pageName = subDomain + ':';
                      
                      if (primaryCategory === 'labs') {
                        pageName += 'f5.labs.v2:labs';
                      } else if (primaryCategory) {
                        pageName += 'f5.' + primaryCategory + '.v2:' + primaryCategory;
                      }
                      
                      // Add remaining path segments
                      for (var i = 1; i < pathParts.length; i++) {
                        pageName += ':' + pathParts[i];
                      }
                      
                      window.digitalData.page.pageInfo.referringPageName = pageName;
                    }
                  } else {
                    // External referrer - simple domain:path format
                    var pathParts = referrerUrl.pathname.split('/').filter(Boolean);
                    var simpleName = hostname + (pathParts.length > 0 ? ':' + pathParts[0] : '');
                    window.digitalData.page.pageInfo.referringPageName = simpleName;
                  }
                } catch (e) {
                  // Invalid URL - leave referringPageName empty
                }
              }

              // Capture utm_content parameter (marketing attribution)
              if (window.digitalData?.user?.userInfo) {
                const urlParams = new URLSearchParams(window.location.search);
                const utmContent = urlParams.get('utm_content');

                if (utmContent) {
                  // digitalSignal format: "device:referrer:utm_content"
                  const existingSignal = window.digitalData.user.userInfo.digitalSignal || '';
                  const parts = existingSignal.split(':');

                  if (parts.length >= 2) {
                    window.digitalData.user.userInfo.digitalSignal = parts[0] + ':' + parts[1] + ':' + utmContent;
                  } else if (parts.length === 1 && parts[0]) {
                    window.digitalData.user.userInfo.digitalSignal = parts[0] + '::' + utmContent;
                  } else {
                    window.digitalData.user.userInfo.digitalSignal = '::' + utmContent;
                  }
                }
              }
            } catch (e) {
              console.error('[F5 Labs Analytics] Browser patch failed:', e);
            }
          })();
        </script><aside id="vital-components-section" class="w-full border border-[#CCCCCC] rounded-[5px] p-[24px] bg-white mb-8 mt-0" aria-label="Vital components"><h3 id="vital-components-header" class="font-heading font-bold text-[20px] leading-[25px] md:text-[28px] md:leading-[35px] text-[#222222] mb-6 tracking-tight">Vital components</h3><div class="space-y-6"><div><h4 class="font-sans font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222] mb-2">Attack Type</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans text-[18px] leading-[27px] text-[#222222] list-none p-0 m-0"><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Client-Side Attacks</li></ul></div><div><h4 class="font-sans font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222] mb-2">Attack Method</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans text-[18px] leading-[27px] text-[#222222] list-none p-0 m-0"><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Client-Platform Malware</li><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Session Hijacking</li><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Phishing</li></ul><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors rounded-[3px] focus-visible:outline-2 focus-visible:outline-offset-2 Motive</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans text-[18px] leading-[27px] text-[#222222] list-none p-0 m-0"><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Cybercrime</li></ul></div><div><h4 class="font-sans font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222] mb-2">Malware Type</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans text-[18px] leading-[27px] text-[#222222] list-none p-0 m-0"><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">Trojan</li></ul></div><div><h4 class="font-sans font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222] mb-2">Malware / Campaign Name</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans text-[18px] leading-[27px] text-[#222222] list-none p-0 m-0"><li class="list-none p-0 m-0">MaliBot</li></ul></div></div></aside><div id="custom-portable-text-rich-text" class="article-portable-text text-grayscale-700"><p class="article-content-paragraph">While tracking the mobile banking trojan <a href="/labs/articles/flubots-authors-employ-creative-and-sophisticated-techniques-to-achieve-their-goals-in-version-50-and-beyond" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">FluBot</a>, F5 Labs recently discovered a new strain of Android malware which we have dubbed “MaliBot”. While its main targets are online banking customers in Spain and Italy, its ability to steal credentials, cookies, and bypass multi-factor authentication (MFA) codes, means that Android users all over the world must be vigilant. Some of MaliBot’s key characteristics include:</p><ul><li>MaliBot disguises itself as a cryptocurrency mining app named “Mining X” or “The CryptoApp”, and occasionally assumes some other guises, such as “MySocialSecurity” and “Chrome”</li><li>MaliBot is focused on stealing financial information, credentials, crypto wallets, and personal data (PII), and also targets financial institutions in Italy and Spain</li><li>Malibot is capable of stealing and bypassing multi-factor (2FA/MFA) codes</li><li>It includes the ability to remotely control infected devices using a VNC server implementation</li></ul><p class="article-content-paragraph">This article is a deep dive into the tactics and techniques this malware strain employs to steal personal data and evade detection.</p><h2 id="e9910ffba8f7" class="article-content-header-2">MaliBot Overview</h2><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot’s command and control (C2) is in Russia and appears to use the same servers that were used to distribute the Sality malware. Many campaigns have originated from this IP since June of 2020 (see Indicators of Compromise). It is a heavily modified re-working of the SOVA malware, with different functionality, targets, C2 servers, domains and packing schemes.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot has an extensive array of features:</p><ul><li>Web injection/overlay attacks</li><li>Theft of cryptocurrency wallets (Binance, Trust)</li><li>Theft of MFA/2FA codes</li><li>Theft of cookies</li><li>Theft of SMS messages</li><li>The ability to by-pass Google two-step authentication</li><li>VNC access to the device and screen capturing</li><li>The ability to run and delete applications on demand</li><li>The ability to send SMS messages on demand</li><li>Information gathering from the device, including its IP, AndroidID, model, language, installed application list, screen and locked states, and reporting on the malware’s own capabilities</li><li>Extensive logging of any successful or failed operations, phone activities (calls, SMS) and any errors</li></ul><h2 id="ebcb5af9b807" class="article-content-header-2">Distributing MaliBot</h2><p class="article-content-paragraph">Distribution of MaliBot is performed by attracting victims to fraudulent websites where they are tricked into downloading the malware, or by directly sending SMS phishing messages (smishing) to mobile phone numbers.</p><h3 id="3efee2acbe94" class="article-content-header-3">Websites</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">The malware authors have so far created two campaigns– “Mining X” and “TheCryptoApp” – each of which has a website with a download link to the malware (see Campaign Screenshots in the Appendix).</p><p class="article-content-paragraph"><strong>TheCryptoApp</strong> campaign attempts to trick people into downloading their malware instead of the legitimate TheCryptoApp – a cryptocurrency tracker app with more than 1 million downloads in the Google Play Store.<a id="mark-footnote_0" href="#article-footnote_0" class="mark-footnote-link" data-tealium-text="1" data-tealium-class="footnote-ref"><sup class="mark-footnote font-bold">1</sup></a></p><p class="article-content-paragraph">For stealth and targeting purposes, the download link will direct the user to the malware APK only if the victim visits the website from an Android device, otherwise, the download link will refer to the real TheCryptoApp app in the play store (see Figure 1 and Figure 2 ).</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>

Javascript that changes the download URL based on the victim’s User-Agent header." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">The <strong>Mining X</strong> campaign is not based on any actual application in the Google Play store, but instead presents a QR code that leads to the malware APK.</p><h3 id="ceb89f1603b4" class="article-content-header-3">Smishing</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">Smishing is commonly used among mobile banker-trojans because it allows the malware to spread in a fast and controllable way, and in this case, MaliBot is no different. MaliBot can send SMS messages on-demand, and once it receives a “sendsms” command containing a text to send and a phone list from the C2 server, MaliBot sends the SMS to each phone number (Figure 3).</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {
  float: left;
  padding-right: 1.5rem;

}

}

}

.image-wrapper .two-third {
  width: 66.66667%;
}

  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-09251ec2f94b9d70d5b5b61abeda2de7108039a0-1803x812-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/09251ec2f94b9d70d5b5b61abeda2de7108039a0-1803x812.png" alt="Figure 3. MaliBot code sending smishing messages to targeted phone numbers." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 3. MaliBot code sending smishing messages to targeted phone numbers.</figcaption></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot’s C2 IP has been used in other malware smishing campaigns since June 2020, which raises questions about how the authors of this malware are related to other campaigns (see Campaign Screenshots).</p><h2 id="db5a45339764" class="article-content-header-2">How MaliBot Works</h2><p class="article-content-paragraph">Android ‘packers’ are becoming increasingly popular with malware developers since they allow native code to be encrypted within the mobile app making reverse engineering and analysis much more difficult. Using the Tencent packer, MaliBot unpacks itself by decrypting an encrypted Dex file from the assets and loading it in runtime using MultiDex. We have a detailed analysis on the Tencent packer in the “Dex decryption” section in our <a href="/labs/articles/flubots-authors-employ-creative-and-sophisticated-techniques-to-achieve-their-goals-in-version-50-and-beyond" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">Flubot article</a>. Please note that not all MaliBot samples are packed.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph">Once loaded, MaliBot contacts the C2 server to register the infected device, then asks the victim to grant accessibility and launcher permissions. MaliBot then registers four services that perform most of the malicious operations:</p><ul><li>Background Service <ul><li>Polls for commands from C2</li><li>Handles C2 commands</li><li>Sends device and malware information (such as permissions enabled, phone locked, &quot;VNC&quot; enabled, etc.)</li><li>Send Keep-Alive pings to C2</li></ul></li><li>Notify Service<ul><li>Checks Accessibility permissions, if not granted it sends a notification to enable these permissions and navigates to Settings.</li></ul></li><li>Accessibility Service <ul><li>Implementing a VNC-like functionality using the Accessibility API (see below)</li><li>Grabbing information from screen</li><li>Populate Bus object which saves device’s states</li></ul></li><li>Screen Capture service<ul><li>Responsible for capturing the screen, also used as part of the &quot;VNC&quot; implementation</li></ul></li></ul><p class="article-content-paragraph">Four Receivers are registered as well:</p><ul><li>SMS Receiver – interception of SMS messages</li><li>Boot Receiver</li><li>Call Receiver</li><li>Alarm receiver – background service watchdog to intercept calls, register boot activity, and intercept alarms.</li></ul><h3 id="8347073afffc" class="article-content-header-3">Accessibility API Abuse</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot performs most of its malicious operations by abusing Android’s Accessibility API. The Accessibility API is a powerful tool developed to encourage Android developers to build apps accessible for users with additional needs. The Accessibility API allows mobile apps to perform actions on behalf of the user, including the ability to read text from the screen, press buttons and listen for other accessibility events.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph">However, these powerful functions can also allow attackers to steal sensitive information and manipulate the device to their advantage. Flubot, Sharkbot and Teabot are just a few examples of banking trojans other than MaliBot that abuse the accessibility API. This service also allows mobile malware to maintain persistence. The malware can protect itself against uninstallation and permissions removal by looking for specific text or labels on the screen and pressing the back button to prevent them.</p><h3 id="e0b0a3a316a6" class="article-content-header-3">Google’s 2-Step Verification Bypass</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">Stealing credentials is often not enough to allow an attacker to successfully log in to a victim’s account. Since Google accounts are often enabled with multifactor authentication (also known as two-factor authentication, or in Google’s case, 2-step verification), a prompt will be shown on the victim’s devices if an unknown device tries to log in. The prompt will ask the victim to grant or deny the login attempt, then match a number shown on the other device. Once they have used MaliBot to capture credentials, the attackers can authenticate to Google accounts on the C2 server using those credentials, and use MaliBot to extract the MFA codes through the following steps:</p><ul><li>First, it validates the current screen is a Google promp﻿t screen (Figure 4 &amp; Figure 5).</li><li>Using the Accessibility API, the malware clicks on the &quot;Yes&quot; button</li><li>The attacker logs the MFA code shown on the attacker’s device to the C2.</li><li>The malware then retrieves the MFA code that was shown on the attacker’s device from the C2 (Figure 6 &amp; Figure 7).</li><li>MaliBot then clicks on the correct button on the screen by matching the buttons’ value against the number retrieved from the C2 server (Figure 8 and Figure 9).</li></ul><h3 id="d0583cb8b857" class="article-content-header-3">Injects</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">When MaliBot registers the device to the C2, it also sends the device’s application list, which helps the C2 determine which overlays/injections to provide whenever an “injectlist” command is sent (Figure 10). The response from the server is a list of apps and their associated injection link (Figure 11). Each injection link contains an HTML overlay that looks identical to the original app. Figure 12-Figure 15 show example injection overlays from financial institutions, in these cases based in Spain and Italy.</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>

C2 response containing injection/overlay links.</figcaption></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot listens for events using the Accessibility Service. MaliBot setting up a WebView to perform the injection.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h3 id="880fc6c3402d" class="article-content-header-3">Stealing: Cookies, MFA Codes, and Wallets</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot’s primary goal is the theft of personal data, credentials and financial information. It has a number of methods to accomplish this, including the ability to steal cookies, multifactor authentication (MFA) codes and crypto wallets.</p><h4 id="89bf1436f91a" class="article-content-header-4">Cookies</h4><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot can steal credentials and cookies of the victim’s Google account. When the victim opens a Google app from the list below, MaliBot opens a WebView to a Google sign-in page (Figure 19). The victim is forced to sign in, as they cannot use the back button to exit the WebView.</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {
  float: left;
  padding-right: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .pull-right {
  float: right;
  padding-left: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .one-third {
  width: 33.33333%;
}

.image-wrapper .half {
  width: 50%;
}

.image-wrapper .two-third {
  width: 66.66667%;
}

.image-wrapper::after {
  clear: both;
  content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-fd1f88bd16005cd7a95a2df6c334c1b820170239-2545x785-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/fd1f88bd16005cd7a95a2df6c334c1b820170239-2545x785.png" alt="Figure 19. Code snippet of process to detect Google app opening." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 19. Code snippet of process to detect Google app opening.</figcaption></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">These are the Google Apps MaliBot monitors and initiates a WebView to collect credentials upon launch:</p><ul><li>com.android.vending</li><li>com.google.android.apps.maps</li><li>com.google.android.gm</li><li>com.google.android.youtube</li><li>com.google.android.apps.photos</li><li>com.google.android.apps.docs</li><li>com.google.android.apps.youtube.music</li><li>com.google.android.music</li></ul><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot uses the “<em>shouldInterceptRequest</em>” WebView function to intercept the URLs that will be loaded to the WebView. By intercepting the URLs of the WebView, MaliBot knows which of four login stages the victim is in:</p><ul><li>https://accounts.google[.]com/signin/v2/identifier - login page</li><li>https://accounts.google[.]com/_/lookup/accountlookup - Checks if Email exists</li><li>https://accounts.google[.]com/_/signin/challenge - MFA challenge page</li><li>https://myaccount.google[.]com – Successful login page</li></ul><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot extracts the email and password entered to the WebView sign-in page using the Accessibility API. Before sending the credentials to the C2 server (Figure 20), MaliBot creates redirections within the WebView to <a href="https://mail.google.com/" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">Gmail</a>, <a href="https://pay.google.com/" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">Google Pay</a>, and <a href="https://passwords.google.com/" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">Google Passwords</a> and tries to grab the cookies of each redirection. MaliBot also uses the Accessibility API to try to capture passwords from Google Passwords.</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>

MaliBot sends email, password, recovery and cookie list to the C2 server.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h4 id="3d1f9d287859" class="article-content-header-4">Google Authenticator MFA Codes</h4><p class="article-content-paragraph">Separately from the Google multifactor authentication bypass listed above, MaliBot is also able to steal multifactor authentication codes from Google Authenticator on-demand. Exfiltration of balance and seed phrases to the C2 server." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 33. Exfiltration of balance and seed phrases to the C2 server.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h3 id="156df0b7896b" class="article-content-header-3">Full Remote Control of Infected Device</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">The Accessibility API of Android allows MaliBot to perform inputs as though it was the victim. It abuses this functionality to implement something akin to a VNC server which allows remote control of the victim’s device. The attacker is able to obtain screen captures from the victim and send input commands to the malware to perform actions. The remote control communicates with a hardcoded IP over HTTP port 1080 (see Indicators of Compromise) and offers several commands (Figure 34):</p><ul><li>back button</li><li>home button</li><li>lock button</li><li>“recents” button</li><li>click (x, y coordinates)</li><li>long press (x, y coordinates)</li><li>swipe (x, y – x, y coordinates)</li><li>scroll (x, y coordinates, amount)</li><li>show/hide overlay</li><li>take screenshot</li><li>paste</li><li>clear text</li><li>start an app</li><li>upload app list</li></ul><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {
  float: left;
  padding-right: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .pull-right {
  float: right;
  padding-left: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .one-third {
  width: 33.33333%;
}

.image-wrapper .half {
  width: 50%;
}

.image-wrapper .two-third {
  width: 66.66667%;
}

.image-wrapper::after {
  clear: both;
  content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-98e796f7d8658e1215d822e97e31c11c8e8f7112-1538x819-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/98e796f7d8658e1215d822e97e31c11c8e8f7112-1538x819.png" alt="Figure 34. Command handling for VNC-like functionality." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 34. Command handling for VNC-like functionality.</figcaption></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">This effectively creates an Accessibility API-based remote access trojan (RAT) that allows the attacker to conveniently access the device remotely.</p><h3 id="823c031e2da3" class="article-content-header-3">SMS Sending</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot can send SMS messages on-demand, which is mostly used for smishing campaigns, but can also be used for monetization by sending a Premium SMS which bills the victim’s mobile credits (if enabled). The malware verifies that it has SEND_SMS permission (Figure 35), waits for the “sendsms” command to be dispatched from the C2 (Figure 36), along with a phone number list and a text to send, and then sends the text to every phone number in the list (Figure 37).</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>

MaliBot checking for SMS permissions." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 35.   content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-aceb0f4ad9526de28d76207e27828afab833f3a9-1679x650-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/aceb0f4ad9526de28d76207e27828afab833f3a9-1679x650.png" alt="Figure 36. Code for MaliBot actions when it receives the &quot;sendsms&quot; command." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 36. Code for MaliBot actions when it receives the &quot;sendsms&quot; command.</figcaption></figure></div></div><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>

}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-f9ce1f3d03926356304dbe34a2d4899a2a12bb72-1699x1311-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/f9ce1f3d03926356304dbe34a2d4899a2a12bb72-1699x1311.png" alt="Figure 37. MaliBot sending SMS messages to every phone number on the list provided by the C2." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 37. MaliBot sending SMS messages to every phone number on the list provided by the C2.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h3 id="477b618bfe9b" class="article-content-header-3">Logging</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot logs any uncaught exceptions, this helps the malware authors to find and fix bugs in their code (Figure 38).</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {

  padding-right: 1.5rem;

}

  width: 50%;

  clear: both;
  content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-a8aeac51f772a7c2258d9ca411b19a4d72b2d2c8-1862x497-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/a8aeac51f772a7c2258d9ca411b19a4d72b2d2c8-1862x497.png" alt="Figure 38. Error logging and reporting in the malware." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 38. Error logging and reporting in the malware.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h2 id="613b84cec42a" class="article-content-header-2">Evasion and Stealth</h2><p class="article-content-paragraph">Malware authors commonly employ tactics to prevent victims from discovering malicious apps, and to make it harder for security researchers to uncover their true purposes. MaliBot currently uses some of these capabilities and appears to have the ability to use more in the future.</p><h3 id="6fc2d50a3592" class="article-content-header-3">Current MaliBot Evasion Techniques</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">Android is able to pause or kill a running service in the background if it’s not active or if the OS needs the resources. To keep the Background Service of the malware alive, MaliBot sets itself as a launcher. Every time the launcher activity is visited (which is very often) it starts or wakes up the service (Figure 39).</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {
  float: left;
  padding-right: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .pull-right {
  float: right;
  padding-left: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .one-third {
  width: 33.33333%;
}

.image-wrapper .half {
  width: 50%;
}

.image-wrapper .two-third {
  width: 66.66667%;
}

.image-wrapper::after {
  clear: both;
  content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-2307087aec66d1ff618bc701c2175db608470d0e-1710x500-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/2307087aec66d1ff618bc701c2175db608470d0e-1710x500.png" alt="Figure 39. MaliBot maintaining its Background Service by observing launcher activity." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 39. MaliBot maintaining its Background Service by observing launcher activity.</figcaption></figure></div></div><p class="article-content-paragraph">This capability also allows the malware to get notified on every launched application and check whether an overlay/injection attack should be performed, according to the list of overlay-ready applications provided by the C2.</p><h3 id="d893f60496a7" class="article-content-header-3">Future Evasion Capabilities</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">The malware authors included functions in MaliBot that are not used in current version. Those functions could potentially be used in later versions, however, it is also possible that the malware authors copied code from somewhere else and not used the entire functionality.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph">One example is a function that can detect if its running in an emulated environment. Another example is a function responsible for setting the malware as a hidden app, so it won’t be visible in the app drawer. Both functions were seen and used in SOVA malware code.</p><div class="image-wrapper flex items-center justify-center my-6"><div class="img-full w-full" style="max-width:990px"><style>
.image-wrapper .pull-left {
  float: left;
  padding-right: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .pull-right {
  float: right;
  padding-left: 1.5rem;
}

.image-wrapper .one-third {
  width: 33.33333%;
}

.image-wrapper .half {
  width: 50%;
}

.image-wrapper .two-third {
  width: 66.66667%;
}

.image-wrapper::after {
  clear: both;
  content: "";
  display: table;
}
</style><figure id="figure-section-image-15d144f59c356ca31ddff34c7f24e557def35b14-2459x2280-png" class="m-0"><img src="https://cdn.studio.f5.com/images/k6fem79d/translations-production/15d144f59c356ca31ddff34c7f24e557def35b14-2459x2280.png" alt="Figure 40. Unused capability in MaliBot that checks various device attributes against hardcoded values to determine whether the device is running in an emulated environment." class="w-full h-auto rounded object-contain"/><figcaption class="article-figure-caption">Figure 40. Unused capability in MaliBot that checks various device attributes against hardcoded values to determine whether the device is running in an emulated environment.</figcaption></figure></div></div><h2 id="3b2adf920b0a" class="article-content-header-2">Conclusion</h2><p class="article-content-paragraph">MaliBot is most obviously a threat to customers of Spanish and Italian banks, but we can expect a broader range of targets to be added to the app as time goes on. In addition, the versatility of the malware and the control it gives attackers over the device mean that it could, in principle, be used for a wider range of attacks than stealing credentials and cryptocurrency. In fact, any application which makes use of WebView is liable to having the users’ credentials and cookies stolen.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph">The F5 Labs <a href="/labs/articles/2022-application-protection-report-in-expectation-of-exfiltration" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors text-primary hover:underline">2022 Application Protection Report</a> also noted that while the rise of ransomware has been the most dramatic attacker trend in the last two years, 2021 also saw a more subtle rise in malware infections that exfiltrated data without pursuing encryption and a ransom. Such a capable and versatile example of mobile malware serves as a reminder that the attack trends <em>du jour</em> are never the only threat worth paying attention to. We hope the following indicators of compromise are helpful for responders in mitigating this threat.</p><p class="article-content-paragraph"><strong>Think we got something wrong? Have questions for the authors? Comments are welcome below!</strong></p><h2 id="73932cc7078f" class="article-content-header-2">Appendix</h2><h3 id="c8d68348d5d3" class="article-content-header-3">Campaign Screenshots</h3><p class="article-content-paragraph">Additional screenshots were captured during the investigation of MaliBot which show previous attacker campaigns (Figure 41) and malicious websites used to trick victims in to downloading the malicious Android app (Figure 42, Figure 43, and Figure 44).</p></div><aside id="article-actions-section" class="w-full border border-[#CCCCCC] rounded-[5px] p-[24px] bg-white my-8" aria-label="Recommendations"><h3 id="article-actions-header" class="font-heading font-bold text-[20px] leading-[25px] md:text-[28px] md:leading-[35px] text-[#222222] mb-6">Recommendations</h3><div class="space-y-6" id="recommendations"><div id="article-actions-group_fcbf360852c1" class="space-y-3"><h4 class="font-heading font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222]">Administrative Preventative</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans font-normal text-[18px] leading-[27px] tracking-normal text-[#222222] list-disc pl-5"><li>User awareness training to educate customers and staff on the dangers of downloading apps from unapproved sources and the risks of enabling Accessibility options.</li></ul></div><div id="article-actions-group_fa470e4bda75" class="space-y-3"><h4 class="font-heading font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222]">Technical Preventative</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans font-normal text-[18px] leading-[27px] tracking-normal text-[#222222] list-disc pl-5"><li>Configure Android devices so that they only download apps from vetted marketplaces, such as Google Play.</li><li>For organizations: ensure that device policies enforce Google Play downloads and consider implementing Android for Work profiles to ensure sandboxing of corporate accounts and data.</li></ul></div><div id="article-actions-group_0398f7abdb10" class="space-y-3"><h4 class="font-heading font-bold text-[18px] leading-[22.5px] md:text-[24px] md:leading-[30px] text-[#222222]">Technical Detective</h4><ul class="space-y-2 font-sans font-normal text-[18px] leading-[27px] tracking-normal text-[#222222] list-disc pl-5"><li>Monitor traffic to known C2 domains or IP address ranges.</li></ul></div></div></aside></article><!--$--><!--/$--><div class="mt-6 flex items-center gap-3 md:hidden"><div class="flex items-center gap-2.5 "><span class="font-sans class="flex-1 min-w-0"><h4 class="font-sans font-bold text-[20px] leading-[25px] tracking-normal text-[#222222]"><a href="/labs/authors/david-warburton" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors rounded-[3px] focus-visible:outline-2 focus-visible:outline-offset-2 focus-visible:outline-solid focus-visible:outline-[var(--primary)] underline decoration-1 underline-offset-4 hover:no-underline active:no-underline text-[var(--primary)] visited:text-[var(--color-eggplant)] hover:text-[var(--color-carbon)] focus-visible:text-[var(--color-carbon)] active:text-[var(--color-steel)]">David Warburton</a></h4><p class="d-body-sm [font-variant-numeric:lining-nums_tabular-nums] text-[#222222] mt-0.5">Director, F5 Labs | F5</p><p class="font-sans font-normal text-[18px] leading-[27px] tracking-normal [font-variant-numeric:lining-nums_tabular-nums] text-[#222222] mt-3">David Warburton is director of F5 Labs. He has worked in the IT industry for over 20 years, starting life as a full stack developer before wrangling with the perils of cloud architecture and then moving the serene and peaceful life of cyber security. His research covers a wide range of topics from the deeply technical, such as cryptography, to the more real-world sociotechnical side of security.

Warburton has given talks around world for Infosec (UK), GovWare (Singapore), Crikeycon (Australia), ITBN (Hungary), Digital Transformation EXPO (UK) and for the UK military. Warburton has made dozens of international media appearances on TV, print and industry podcasts, including BBC World News, Sky News, SABC, The Guardian and Risky Business. He is the author of F5 Labs publications including the annual ‘Phishing and Fraud’ and ‘TLS Telemetry’ reports, and recently he co-authored the SSL/TLS/HTTPS scanning devops tool ‘Cryptonice’ which helps organisations improve their application security posture.

Warburton was awarded a Masters in Information Security from Royal Holloway University of London where his thesis was on the use of security and cryptography in internet-of-things (IoT).</p><a href="/labs/authors/david-warburton" class="cursor-pointer transition-colors rounded-[3px] focus-visible:outline-2 focus-visible:outline-offset-2 focus-visible:outline-solid
